 The combination of Planck and BK15 robustly constrain the tensor ratio to be small, with r 0.002 ≲ 0.06 r_{0.002}\la 0.06 .
The implications for inflation are slightly more model dependent as a result of
degeneracies between n s n_{\rm s} and additional parameters in extended Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} models. However, as shown in Table 5 ,
the extensions of Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} that we consider in this paper cannot substantially shift the value of the spectral index when the tensor amplitude is small, so the overall conclusions are unlikely to change substantially in extended models. 
 7.3 Spatial curvature 
 Figure 29: 
Constraints on a non-flat universe as a minimal extension to the base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model.
Points show samples from the Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE chains coloured by the value of the Hubble parameter and with transparency proportional to the sample weight.
Dashed lines show the corresponding 68 % 68\thinspace\% and 95 % 95\thinspace\% confidence contours that close away from the flat model (vertical line), while
dotted lines are the equivalent contours from the alternative CamSpec likelihood.
The solid dashed line shows the constraint from adding Planck lensing, which pulls the result back towards consistency with flat (within 2 ​ σ 2\thinspace\sigma ). The filled contour shows the result of also adding BAO data, which makes the full joint constraint very consistent with a flat universe.
 The base- Λ \Lambda CDM model assumes that the spatial hypersurfaces are flat,
such as would be predicted (to within measurable precision) by the simplest inflationary models.
This is a prediction that can be tested to high accuracy by the combination
of CMB and BAO data (the CMB alone suffers from a geometric degeneracy, which is
weakly broken with the addition of CMB lensing). This is illustrated in Fig. 29 . 
 The combination of the Planck temperature and polarization power spectra
give 
 Ω K = − 0.056 − 0.018 + 0.028 (68 %,  Planck  TT+lowE), \left.\Omega_{K}=-0.056^{+0.028}_{-0.018}\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{142.26378pt}{\begin{flushleft}(68\thinspace\%,\penalty\ {Planck}\ TT\allowbreak+lowE), \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (44a) 
 Ω K = − 0.044 − 0.015 + 0.018 (68 %,  Planck  TT,TE,EE+lowE), \left.\Omega_{K}=-0.044^{+0.018}_{-0.015}\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{142.26378pt}{\begin{flushleft}(68\thinspace\%,\penalty\ {Planck}\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE), \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (44b) 
 an apparent detection of curvature at well over 2 ​ σ 2\thinspace\sigma .
The 99 % 99\thinspace\% probability region for the TT,TE,EE+lowE result is − 0.095 < Ω K < − 0.007 -0.095<\Omega_{K}<-0.007 , with only about 1/10000 samples at Ω K ≥ 0 \Omega_{K}\geq 0 . This is not entirely a volume effect, since the best-fit χ 2 \chi^{2} changes by Δ ​ χ eff 2 = − 11 \Delta\chi^{2}_{\rm eff}=-11 compared to base Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} when adding the one additional curvature parameter. The reasons for the pull towards negative values of Ω K \Omega_{K} are
discussed at length in 284 and Sect. 6.2 . They are essentially the
same as those that lead to the preference for A L > 1 A_{\rm L}>1 ,
although slightly exacerbated in the case of curvature, since the low multipoles also fit the low- ℓ \ell temperature likelihood slightly better if Ω K < 0 \Omega_{K}<0 .
As with the A L > 1 A_{\rm L}>1 preference, the joint Planck polarization result is not robust at the approximately 0.5 ​ σ 0.5\thinspace\sigma level to modelling of the polarization likelihoods, with the CamSpec TT,TE,EE+lowE likelihood giving Ω K = − 0.037 − 0.014 + 0.019 \Omega_{K}=-0.037^{+0.019}_{-0.014} . 
 Closed models predict substantially higher lensing amplitudes than in Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} ,
so combining with the lensing reconstruction (which is
consistent with a flat model) pulls
parameters back into consistency with a spatially flat universe to well within 2 ​ σ 2\thinspace\sigma : 
 Ω K = − 0.0106 ± 0.0065 (68 %, TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing). \left.\Omega_{K}=-0.0106\pm 0.0065\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{113.81102pt}{\begin{flushleft}(68\thinspace\%,\penalty\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE\allowbreak+lensing). \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (45a) 
 The constraint can be further sharpened by combining the Planck data with
BAO data; this convincingly breaks the geometric degeneracy to give 
 Ω K = 0.0007 ± 0.0019 (68 %, TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO). \left.\Omega_{K}=0.0007\pm 0.0019\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{113.81102pt}{\begin{flushleft}(68\thinspace\%,\penalty\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE\allowbreak+lensing\allowbreak+BAO). \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (45b) 
 The joint results suggests our Universe is spatially flat to a 1 ​ σ 1\thinspace\sigma accuracy of
 0.2 % 0.2\thinspace\% . 
 7.4 Dark energy and modified gravity 
 The late-time accelerated expansion of the Universe ( Riess et al. 1998 ; Perlmutter et al. 1999 ) is still considered one of the most mysterious
aspects of the standard cosmology. In the base Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model the
acceleration is driven by a cosmological constant, added into the
Einstein equations of General Relativity ( Einstein 1917 , GR,) .
Although Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} fits the data well, Λ \Lambda is a phenomenological
parameter without an underlying theoretical basis to explain its
value ( Weinberg 1987 , though see) . In addition, the
empirically required value of Λ \Lambda marks our epoch as a special
time in the evolution of the Universe. Attempts have therefore been
made to find a dynamical mechanism that leads to cosmic acceleration,
with evolving background energy densities close to Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} . Such dynamics is
usually associated with a fluid (a scalar field) which we refer to as
“dark energy” (DE), or with modifications of GR, which we refer to
as “modified gravity” (MG). 
 A detailed analysis of the impact of Planck data on dark energy and
modified gravity was presented in a dedicated paper that accompanied
the 2015 Planck release, ( Planck Collaboration
XIV 2016 , hereafter 285 ) .
We refer the reader to this paper for a review of different
cosmological models, and for constraints
from Planck on its own and in combination with galaxy weak lensing
(WL) and redshift-space distortions (RSDs). In 285 it was shown
that although the base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model fits Planck data, there were some
tensions (at levels as high as 3 ​ σ 3\thinspace\sigma ) when Planck was
combined with RSD and WL data, even when conservative cuts were applied to
exclude nonlinear scales. However, the addition
of Planck lensing data was found to reduce these tensions. Updated
constraints on a few specific models, using more recent WL data, are
presented in DES Collaboration 2018b . 
 In this paper, we follow a similar methodology to 285 ,
distinguishing between models that directly affect only the background
(and impact perturbations predominantly through changes in the
expansion rate) and those that directly affect perturbations.
However, we restrict the analysis to a smaller range of models here. As in
the rest of this paper, we show results for the
baseline Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing data set and for combinations with other
relevant data sets. Such external data are particularly useful for
constraining DE and MG models because the largest deviations
from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} are usually at late times, which are not well constrained
by the CMB power-spectra and CMB lensing. However, CMB lensing
provides important information that mitigates the preference for
 A L >  1 A_{\rm L}\thinspace{>}\thinspace 1 seen in the Planck temperature power spectra
(Sect. 6.2 ), so we explicitly comment on the impact of CMB lensing
wherever relevant. We recall here that the lensing likelihood assumes
a fiducial Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model, but linear corrections to the fiducial mode
are accounted for self-consistently. 294 explicitly tested
that this procedure is unbiased, even when the lensing spectrum differs
from the fiducial spectrum by as much as 20 % (which is much larger
than differences allowed by the CMB lensing data). 
 We consider the following external data sets: 
 • 
 SNe + BAO (see Sects. 5.1 , 5.2 , and 5.4 
for discussions of the data sets and
comments on why we do not combine Planck data with direct measurements of
 H 0 H_{0} ); 
 • 
 RSDs (as described in Sect. 5.3 ), where we specifically use
BOSS-DR12 data from Alam et al. 2017 , adopting the f ​ σ 8 f\sigma_{8} – H H – D M D_{\rm M} 
parameterization; 
 • 
 WL data from DES (as described in Sect. 5.5 ),
except that here we use the Weyl potential to obtain theoretical predictions for the lensing correlation functions, rather than assuming the matter-sourced Poisson equation to relate the
lensing potential power spectrum to the matter power spectrum. 
 We calculate all results both fixing and varying the neutrino mass.
Neutrino masses are known
to be degenerate with DE and MG and should be varied consistently
when testing such models ( Dirian 2017 , as discussed in) ; fixing the
neutrino mass to the minimal value of 0.06 ​ eV 0.06\thinspace{\rm eV} (as for our baseline Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} results)
gives tighter constraints than allowing the neutrino mass
to vary and partly shifts
results towards Λ \Lambda CDM. These shifts are usually small, often
negligible, and always less than 1 ​ σ 1\thinspace\sigma for marginalized
results.
We model the small-scale nonlinear power spectrum using HMcode ( Mead et al. 2015 ; Mead et al. 2016 ) as in the main parameter grid of extensions to base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} , neglecting any differences arising from modified gravity. In using the DES weak-lensing correlation functions, we exclude scales where nonlinear modelling uncertainties are important, but since the modified gravity models introduce an additional level of uncertainty, we also marginalize over the feedback amplitude B B with a flat prior, 2 ≤ B ≤ 4 2\leq B\leq 4 . This parameter is used by HMcode to introduce an additional uncertainty in the nonlinear correction due to the modelling of the baryonic effects on the matter power spectrum at small scales, modifying the halo mass-concentration relation and the shape of the halo density profile. In this context, however, we marginalize over this parameter in order to reduce the residual sensitivity of our results on the nonlinear modelling in modified gravity theories; marginalizing over B B reduces the constraining power coming from nonlinear scales, where the correction recipe used by HMcode may not correctly reproduce the perturbation evolution for all the models included in our parameterization. 
 Throughout this section we will adopt the metric given by the line
element 
 d ​ s 2 = a 2 ​ [ − ( 1 + 2 ​ Ψ ) ​ d ​ τ 2 + ( 1 − 2 ​ Φ ) ​ d ​ x 2 ] , ds^{2}=a^{2}\left[-(1+2\Psi)d\tau^{2}+(1-2\Phi)dx^{2}\right]\thinspace, 
 (46) 
 with the speed of light c c set to 1.
The functions Φ ⁡ ( τ , x ) \Phi(\tau,x) and Ψ ⁡ ( τ , x ) \Psi(\tau,x) are the gauge-invariant gravitational potentials, which are very nearly equal at late times in Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} .
For the background parameterization we use the standard CAMB code,
while for the perturbation parameterization we use the publicly available code MGCAMB 33 33 
 33 
 Available at
 http://www.sfu.ca/~aha25/MGCAMB.html (February 2014 version, but updated to correctly output the power spectrum of the Weyl potential). 
 ( Zhao et al. 2009 ; Hojjati et al. 2011 ) integrated into the latest version of CosmoMC .
For the effective field theory (EFT) models of Sect. 7.4.3 we use EFTCAMB 34 34 
 34 
 Available at
 http://eftcamb.org/ (version 2.0). ( Hu et al. 2014 ; Raveri et al. 2014 ) . 
 7.4.1 Background parameterization: w 0 w_{0} , w a w_{a} 
 If the DE is a generic dynamical fluid, its equation of state
parameter w ≡ p / ρ w\equiv p/\rho will in general be a function of time.
Here p p and ρ \rho are the
spatially-averaged (background) DE pressure and density. 
 To test a time-varying equation of state we adopt the functional form 
 w ⁡ ( a ) = w 0 + ( 1 − a ) ​ w a , w(a)=w_{0}+(1-a)w_{a}\thinspace, 
 (47) 
 where w 0 w_{0} and w a w_{a} are assumed to be constants. In Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} , w 0 = − 1 w_{0}=-1 and w a = 0 w_{a}=0 .
We use the parameterized post-Friedmann (PPF) model of Fang et al. 2008 
to explore expansion histories where w w crosses − 1 -1 .
The PPF equations are modelled on the perturbations of quintessence dark energy, i.e., they correspond
to a fluid with vanishing anisotropic stress and a rest-frame speed of sound approximately equal to the speed of light.
Because of the high sound speed, dark-energy density perturbations are suppressed inside the horizon and are irrelevant
compared to the matter perturbations, except on the very largest scales.
While this is the standard procedure adopted in the literature, we should emphasize that a single minimally-coupled canonical scalar field (quintessence) cannot cross w = − 1 w=-1 ( Vikman 2005 ) . Such a crossing could happen in models with two scalar fields (one of which would have to be a phantom field with the opposite sign of the kinetic term); in such models
the perturbations remain close to the quintessence case ( Kunz & Sapone 2006 , see e.g.,) . Alternatively,
the phantom “barrier” can be crossed with a sound speed that vanishes in the phantom domain ( Creminelli et al. 2009 ) 
or in models with additional terms in the action, such as in kinetic-gravity-braiding ( Deffayet et al. 2010 ) , or with non-minimal couplings ( Amendola 2000 ; Pettorino & Baccigalupi 2008 ) . These and other modified gravity models, typically also change the behaviour of
the perturbations. 
 Figure 30: Marginalized posterior distributions of the ( w 0 , w a w_{0},w_{a} )
parameters for various data combinations.
The tightest constraints come from the combination Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+SNe+BAO and are compatible
with Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} . Using Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing alone is considerably less constraining and allows for an area in parameter space that corresponds to large values of the Hubble constant (as already discussed in Planck Collaboration
XIII 2016 and 285 ).
The dashed lines indicate the point corresponding to the Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model. The parametric equation of state given by Eq. ( 47 ) stays out of the phantom regime (i.e., has w ≥ − 1 w\geq-1 ) at all times only in the (upper-right) unshaded region.
 Marginalized contours of the posterior distributions for w 0 w_{0} and
 w a w_{a} are shown in Fig. 30 . Note that CMB lensing has
only a small effect on the constraints from Planck alone (see the
parameter grid tables in the 273 ). Using Planck data alone, a wide volume
of dynamical dark-energy parameter space is allowed, with contours cut
off by our priors ( − 3 < w 0 < 1 -3<w_{0}<1 , − 5 < w a < 5 -5<w_{a}<5 , and 0.4 < h < 1 0.4<h<1 ; note that
Fig. 30 does not show the complete prior range).
However, most of the allowed region of parameter space corresponds to
phantom models with very high values of H 0 H_{0} (as discussed
in 285 ); such models are inconsistent with the late-time evolution
constrained by SNe and BAO data. This is illustrated in Fig. 30 
which also shows constraints if we add BAO/RSD+WL and BAO+SNe to the Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing likelihood. The addition of external data sets narrows the constraints towards the
 Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} values of w 0 = − 1 w_{0}=-1 , w a = 0 w_{a}=0 . The tightest constraints are found for the
data combination Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO+SNe; the difference in χ 2 \chi^{2} 
between the best-fit DE and Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} models for this data combination is
only Δ ​ χ 2 = − 1.4 \Delta\chi^{2}=-1.4 (which is not significant given the two
additional parameters). Numerical constraints for these data
combinations, as well as χ 2 \chi^{2} differences, are presented in
Table 6 . It is also apparent that for the
simple w 0 w_{0} , w a w_{a} parameterization of evolving DE, Planck combined with
external data sets does not allow significantly lower values of S 8 S_{8} or
higher values of H 0 H_{0} compared to the base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} cosmology. 
 Fixing the evolution parameter w a = 0 w_{a}=0 , we obtain the tight constraint 
 w 0 = − 1.028 ± 0.031 (68 %,  Planck  TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+SNe+BAO), \left.w_{0}=-1.028\pm 0.031\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{142.26378pt}{\begin{flushleft}(68\thinspace\%,\penalty\ {Planck}\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE\allowbreak+lensing\allowbreak+{SNe}\allowbreak+BAO), \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (48) 
 and restricting to w 0 > − 1 w_{0}>-1 (i.e., not allowing phantom equations of state), we find 
 w 0 < − 0.95 (95 %,  Planck  TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+SNe+BAO). \left.w_{0}<-0.95\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{156.49014pt}{\begin{flushleft}(95\thinspace\%,\penalty\ {Planck}\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE\allowbreak+lensing\allowbreak+{SNe}\allowbreak+BAO). \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (49) 
 Here we only quote two significant figures, so that the result is robust to differences between
the Plik and CamSpec likelihoods. 
 For the remainder of this section, we assume Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} at the background level (i.e., w = − 1 w=-1 at all times), but instead turn our attention to constraining the behaviour of the dark sector perturbations.

 7.4.2 Perturbation parameterization: μ \mu , η \eta 
 Figure 31: Top : Marginalized posterior distributions of the MG parameters μ \mu and η \eta for Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing data alone and in combination with external data (as indicated in the legend), using the late-time parameterization and neglecting any scale dependence. The dashed lines show the standard Λ \Lambda CDM model. Bottom : Impact of the BAO/RSD and Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE data, compared to the 2015 results. For the 2018 Planck data, the contours shift towards lower values of η 0 − 1 \eta_{0}-1 , along the maximum degeneracy line (black versus cyan contours) and shift in the same direction when using the BAO/RSD data (yellow versus black contours).
 In the types of DE or MG models considered here, changes to observables only arise via the impact on the geometry of the Universe.
At the level of perturbations,
it is then sufficient to model the impact on the gravitational potentials Φ \Phi and
 Ψ \Psi , or, equivalently, on two independent
combinations of these potentials
 ( Zhang et al. 2007 ; Amendola et al. 2008 , e.g.,) .
Following 285 we consider two phenomenological functions, μ \mu and η \eta , defined as follows. 
 1. 
 μ ⁡ ( a , k ) \mu(a,k) : a modification of the Poisson equation for Ψ \Psi , 
 k 2 ​ Ψ = − μ ⁡ ( a , k ) ​  4 ​ π ​ G ​ a 2 ​ [ ρ ​ Δ + 3 ​ ( ρ + P ) ​ σ ] , k^{2}\Psi=-\mu(a,k)\thinspace 4\pi Ga^{2}\left[\rho\Delta+3(\rho+P)\sigma\right], 
 (50) 
 where ρ ​ Δ = ρ m ​ Δ m + ρ r ​ Δ r \rho\Delta=\rho_{\rm m}\Delta_{\rm m}+\rho_{\rm r}\Delta_{\rm r} , using comoving fractional density perturbations Δ \Delta , and where σ \sigma is the anisotropic stress from relativistic species (photons and neutrinos). 
 2. 
 η ⁡ ( a , k ) \eta(a,k) : an effective additional anisotropic
stress, leading to a difference between the gravitational potentials Φ \Phi and Ψ \Psi , defined implicitly through 
 k 2 ​ [ Φ − η ⁡ ( a , k ) ​ Ψ ] = μ ⁡ ( a , k ) ​  12 ​ π ​ G ​ a 2 ​ ( ρ + P ) ​ σ . k^{2}\left[\Phi-\eta(a,k)\Psi\right]=\mu(a,k)\thinspace 12\pi Ga^{2}(\rho+P)\sigma. 
 (51) 
 At late times, σ \sigma from standard particles is negligible and we find 
 η ⁡ ( a , k ) ≈ Φ / Ψ . \eta(a,k)\approx\Phi/\Psi. 
 (52) 
 These definitions are phenomenological, in the sense that they are not
derived from a theoretical action. However, they are able to capture a
generic deviation of the perturbation evolution from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} that does
not need to correspond to a known model. This approach is
complementary to constraints on action-based models, which are the
topic of the next subsection. When η = μ = 1 \eta=\mu=1 we recover GR at all
times, including when there are non-zero contribution from photons and
neutrinos to the density perturbation or anisotropic stress. In the
parameterization adopted here (described further below), the MG
contribution to η \eta is only relevant at late times, when the
anisotropic stress from relativistic particles is negligible. 
 In this section we fix the background evolution to that of Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} ( w = − 1 w=-1 at all times), so
that any significant deviation of μ \mu or η \eta from unity would indicate a deviation from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} .
We also consider constraints on the derived quantity Σ \Sigma , defined as 
 k 2 ​ [ Φ + Ψ ] = − Σ ⁡ ( a , k ) ​ 4 ​ π ​ G ​ a 2 ​ [ 2 ​ ρ ​ Δ − 3 ​ ( ρ + P ) ​ σ ] . k^{2}\left[\Phi+\Psi\right]=-\Sigma(a,k)4\pi Ga^{2}\left[2\rho\Delta-3(\rho+P)\sigma\right]. 
 (53) 
 Since Σ \Sigma measures deviations of the lensing potential from the GR prediction, it is better constrained by WL data than μ \mu and η \eta separately. 
 For simplicity we only allow μ \mu and η \eta to vary with time (as in 285 ). Scale dependence
increases the number of degeneracies in parameter space and may require, for example, higher-order statistics in WL observables ( Peel et al. 2018 ) to break the degeneracies.
We use the late-time DE parameterization of 285 and Casas et al. 2017 , where the time evolution of all quantities is assumed to be proportional to the relative dark-energy density: 
 μ ⁡ ( z ) = 1 + E 11 ​ Ω DE ​ ( z ) ; \mu(z)=1+E_{11}\Omega_{\rm DE}(z); 
 (54a) 
 η ⁡ ( z ) = 1 + E 21 ​ Ω DE ​ ( z ) . \eta(z)=1+E_{21}\Omega_{\rm DE}(z). 
 (54b) 
 This defines the constants E 11 E_{11} and E 21 E_{21} . We report results in terms of μ 0 ≡ μ ⁡ ( z = 0 ) \mu_{0}\equiv\mu(z=0) and η 0 ≡ η ⁡ ( z = 0 ) \eta_{0}\equiv\eta(z=0) , which are determined from E 11 E_{11} and E 21 E_{21} , given the dark-energy density parameter today.
This parameterization is motivated by the assumption that the impact of dark energy depends on its density and
therefore allows for more deviation of μ \mu and η \eta from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} at late times. The alternative early-time parameterization included in 285 led to similar results and is not discussed here for brevity. Our choice of parameterization, of course,
limits the nature of possible deviations from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} ; however, the choices of Eqs. ( 54a ) and ( 54b )
allow us to compare our results directly with those of 285 .

by definition, as discussed in Sect. 6.2 . 
 Figure 31 shows the marginalized constraints on μ 0 \mu_{0} 
and η 0 \eta_{0} from different combinations of data, and also compares
with the results from 285 . Marginalized mean values and errors for
cosmological parameters are presented in Table 7 . This table also lists
results for ⟨ d 2 ⟩ 1 / 2 \langle d^{2}\rangle^{1/2} , the root-mean-square CMB lensing deflection angle, and the parameter combination
 Σ 0 ​ S 8 \Sigma_{0}S_{8} that is well-constrained by the DES WL data. These quantities allow the reader to assess the impact of
lensing data on the parameter constraints.
The μ \mu parameter affects the growth of structure, so, for example, higher μ \mu gives larger values of σ 8 \sigma_{8} . The CMB only constrains MG via the integrated Sachs-Wolfe effect (on large scales, where there is large cosmic variance) and CMB lensing. Lensing observations do not constrain the fluctuation amplitude directly, but the amplitude scaled by Σ \Sigma (as defined in Eq. 53 ). The degeneracy direction shown in Fig. 31 corresponds to approximately constant lensing amplitude, with higher μ 0 \mu_{0} requiring lower Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} and hence lower η 0 \eta_{0} . The thickness of the degeneracy contour and its location depends on the constraint on lensing. With
 Planck data alone, or Planck +SNe+BAO, the lensing amplitude is pulled to high values by the preference for more lensing discussed in Sect. 6.2 , so the contours are slightly shifted with respect to Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} . The inclusion of
WL 35 35 
 35 
 Tests during the writing of this paper revealed a bug in
 MGCAMB that was also present in 2015. This bug reduced the
constraining power of WL data for the ( μ , η \mu,\eta ) parameterization
(which in 2015 was suppressed by the very conservative excision of
nonlinear scales). The CMB and BAO/RSD constraints and other
cosmological models were not affected by this bug. 
data shrinks the contour, and reduces the offset with respect to Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} ;
DES WL data disfavour higher lensing amplitudes than predicted by the Planck Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} cosmology. DES also measures lensing at much lower redshift than CMB lensing, so it is a more powerful probe of MG models where changes to GR only appear at late times (as we have assumed). 
 The BAO/RSD data constrain μ 0 \mu_{0} directly, since redshift distortions are a probe of structure growth. The lower panel of
Fig. 31 shows constraints with BAO/RSD alone, and also demonstrates that removing the CMB lensing reconstruction data shifts the contour further from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} ; this is consistent with the pull away from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} being driven by the preference for more lensing in the high- ℓ \ell CMB power spectra. 
 We can further demonstrate the effect of CMB lensing by varying the consistency parameter A L A_{\rm L} within MG models.
Figure 32 shows the degeneracy between A L A_{\rm L} and Σ 0 − 1 \Sigma_{0}-1 , which is computed as
a derived parameter in our ( μ , η \mu,\eta ) parameterization. Here A L A_{\rm L} affects lensing of the CMB power spectra
only, while Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} encodes modifications to the lensing
amplitude caused by modifications of gravity. The contours show that
MG models ( Σ 0 ≠ 1 \Sigma_{0}\neq 1 ) are preferred by the Planck power spectra (although not strongly) if A L = 1 A_{\rm L}=1 .
The preference for higher Σ 0 \Sigma_{0} values is reduced by allowing
larger A L A_{\rm L} ; the preference for MG that we find is therefore largely another
reflection of the preference for A L > 1 A_{\rm L}>1 discussed in Sect. 6.2 . Adding Planck CMB lensing measurements shifts the contours back into consistency
with Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} (blue contours). Adding BAO/RSD + WL tightens the constrains (red contours)
which remain consistent with Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} .
Using the CamSpec likelihood gives slightly less preference for high A L A_{\rm L} , and the results for Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing shift by about 0.2 ​ σ 0.2\thinspace\sigma towards better consistency with Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} . 
 7.4.3 Effective field theory description of dark energy 
 To investigate action-based models that can give interesting values of
 μ \mu and η \eta , we limit ourselves to a sub-class of effective field
theories
 ( Cheung et al. 2008 ; Creminelli et al. 2009 ; Gubitosi et al. 2013 , EFTs,) . The
EFTs we consider contain models with a single scalar field and at most
second-order equations of motion, a restriction that is in general
necessary to avoid the so-called Ostrogradski instability. In addition,
EFTs typically assume a universal coupling to gravity; models with
non-universal couplings ( Amendola 2000 ; Pettorino & Baccigalupi 2008 ) ,
multiple scalar fields, additional vector ( Hellings & Nordtvedt 1973 ) or
tensor fields ( Hassan et al. 2012 ) , or non-local
models ( Belgacem et al. 2018 ) do not fall into this class and are
not considered here. Nevertheless EFTs provide a general set of models
for which we can, in principle, compute all quantities of interest,
including μ \mu and η \eta (which will span a restricted part of the
 μ \mu – η \eta space considered in the previous section.) 
 As described in section 5.2.1 of 285 , the degrees of freedom in
actions of this class of models can be reduced to the expansion rate
 H H and five additional functions of time ( Gleyzes et al. 2013 ; Bellini & Sawicki 2014 ) 
{ α M , α K , α B , α T , α H \alpha_{\rm M},\alpha_{\rm K},\alpha_{\rm B},\alpha_{\rm T},\alpha_{\rm H} }.
However, measurements of the speed of gravitational waves ( Abbott et al. 2017 ) imply that α T ​ ( z =  0 ) ≃ 0 \alpha_{T}(z\thinspace{=}\thinspace 0)\simeq 0 , which reduces the space of acceptable models ( Lombriser & Taylor 2016 ; McManus et al. 2016 ; Creminelli & Vernizzi 2017 ; Ezquiaga & Zumalacarregui 2017 ; Sakstein & Jain 2017 ; Baker et al. 2017 ) . 
 Apart from models where gravitational wave propagation is not modified
at all, which would necessarily limit us to
 η = 1 \eta=1 ( Saltas et al. 2014 ) , only conformally (non-minimally)
coupled models 36 36 
 36 
 Recently the EFT action has been extended
to include degenerate higher-order theories ( Zumalacárregui & García-Bellido 2014 ; Gleyzes et al. 2015 ; Ben Achour et al. 2016 , DHOST,
) , which
feature an additional parameter called β 1 \beta_{\rm 1} ( Langlois et al. 2017 ) . DHOST models can also give
 α T ≈ 0 \alpha_{\rm T}\approx 0 , but α H \alpha_{\rm H} and β 1 \beta_{\rm 1} are
constrained to be small from astrophysical tests of
gravity ( Crisostomi & Koyama 2018 ; Langlois et al. 2018 ; Dima & Vernizzi 2018 ; Saltas et al. 2018 ) . 
with α B = − α M \alpha_{\rm B}=-\alpha_{\rm M} (and α H = 0 \alpha_{\rm H}=0 ) naturally lead to
 α T = 0 \alpha_{\rm T}=0 . For these reasons we focus on this latter class of
models (and for simplicity we assume α T ​ ( z ) ≃ 0 \alpha_{\rm T}(z)\simeq 0 at all
times), and in addition choose the kinetic terms of the scalar (set by
 α K \alpha_{\rm K} ) to keep the scalar sound speed equal to the speed of light
(current observational data are not able to constrain the sound speed
significantly, see e.g., the k k -essence model constraints
in 285 ). We finally end up with a non-minimally coupled k k -essence
model described by the single function α M \alpha_{\rm M} that determines the
running of the Planck mass. The background expansion is chosen to be
the same as in Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} , as in our analysis of the μ \mu – η \eta parameterization described in the previous
section. The
main difference relative to 285 is that we now allow for
 α M < 0 \alpha_{\rm M}<0 , which corresponds to a Planck mass decreasing with time. 
 Figure 33: Marginalized posterior distribution of Ω 0 EFT \Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{EFT}} that parameterizes the evolution of the Planck mass according to Eq. ( 55 ) in the EFT model. We show constraints
for Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing data (solid lines), as well as Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE data without CMB lensing (dashed lines), both alone and in combination with WL+BAO/RSD data. The Λ \Lambda CDM limit lies at Ω 0 EFT = 0 \Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{EFT}}=0 (vertical dashed line).
 As in 285 we adopt the parameterization α M = α M ​ 0 ​ a β \alpha_{M}=\alpha_{M0}a^{\thinspace\beta} , where α M ​ 0 \alpha_{M0} is the value of α M \alpha_{M} today and
where β > 0 \beta>0 determines how quickly the absolute value of
 α M \alpha_{M} decreases at high redshift. In terms of the non-minimal
coupling function Ω \Omega multiplying the Ricci scalar R R in the
action, this corresponds to 37 37

 This notation is conventional; note that Ω EFT \Omega^{\mathrm{EFT}} here is not the contribution to the critical density, and Ω 0 EFT \Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{EFT}} is not the value of Ω EFT ​ ( a ) \Omega^{\mathrm{EFT}}(a) at a = 1 a=1 . 
 Ω EFT ​ ( a ) = exp ⁡ { α M ​ 0 β ​ a β } − 1 = exp ⁡ { Ω 0 EFT ​ a β } − 1 , \Omega^{\mathrm{EFT}}(a)=\exp\left\{\frac{\alpha_{M0}}{\beta}a^{\thinspace\beta}\right\}-1=\exp\left\{\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{EFT}}a^{\thinspace\beta}\right\}-1\thinspace, 
 (55) 
 which agrees with the exponential model built-in to EFTCAMB ( Raveri et al. 2014 ) (which we use to compute the model
predictions presented here). The resulting posterior distribution on
 Ω 0 EFT \Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{EFT}} , marginalized over β \beta and other
parameters, is shown in Fig. 33 . The Λ \Lambda CDM limit
lies at Ω 0 EFT = 0 \Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{EFT}}=0 (vertical dashed line). We see that
the posterior distribution prefers negative values of
 Ω 0 EFT \Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{EFT}} , with a shift of 1.6 ​ σ 1.6\thinspace\sigma for the
baseline Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing likelihood and 2.1 ​ σ 2.1\thinspace\sigma if CMB lensing
is excluded. These shifts are reduced to 0.8 ​ σ 0.8\thinspace\sigma with the

if Planck lensing is excluded.
Table 8 gives the parameter constraints for these data combinations and lists
the changes in χ 2 \chi^{2} of the best fits relative to base Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} .
As was the case for the ( μ , η \mu,\eta ) parameterization, DES WL measurements
pull the contours towards Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} . If we determine μ 0 \mu_{0} and η 0 \eta_{0} that
correspond to the mean values of the EFT parameters for a specific
scale choice, we find that the parameters lie in the top-left quadrant of
the ( μ , η \mu,\eta ) parameter space shown in Fig. 31 .
Another class of models that predicts values of ( μ , η \mu,\eta ) in the top-left quadrant of Fig. 31 are the non-local models, specifically the RR model of Dirian et al. 2016 ; these models are not discussed here. 
 Overall, the EFT sub-class of non-minimally coupled k k -essence models considered here is
not preferred by current data. Without using CMB and galaxy WL lensing, Planck gives a
moderate preference for models that predict more lensing compared to Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} (as found in
our investigation of the ( μ , η \mu,\eta ) parameterization). However, combining Planck with CMB and DES WL
lensing measurements disfavours high lensing amplitudes and pulls the parameters towards Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} . 
 7.4.4 General remarks 
 Planck alone provides relatively weak constraints on dark energy and modified gravity, but Planck does constrain other cosmological parameters extremely well. By combining Planck with external data we then obtain tight constraints on these
models. We find no strong evidence for deviations from Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} , either at the background level or when allowing for changes to the perturbations. At the background level, Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} is close to the best fit. In the simple μ \mu – η \eta and EFT parameterizations of perturbation-level deviations from GR, we do find better fits to the Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE data compared to Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} ,
but this is largely associated with the preference in the CMB power spectra for higher lensing amplitudes (as discussed in Sect. 6.2 ), rather than a distinctive preference for modified gravity. Adding weak lensing data disfavours the large lensing amplitudes and our results are consistent with Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} to within 1 ​ σ 1\thinspace\sigma .
Since neutrino masses are in general degenerate with DE and MG parameters, it is also worth testing the impact of varying neutrino masses versus fixing them to our base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} value of m ν = 0.06 m_{\nu}=0.06  eV. We find similar trends, with slightly
larger posteriors when varying the neutrino mass. 
 7.5 Neutrinos and extra relativistic species 
 7.5.1 Neutrino masses 
 The Planck base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model assumes a normal mass hierarchy
with the minimal mass ∑ m ν = 0.06 \sum m_{\nu}=0.06\thinspace eV allowed by neutrino flavour oscillation experiments. However, current observations are consistent with many neutrino mass models, and there are no compelling theoretical reasons to
strongly prefer any one of them. Since the masses are already known to be non-zero, allowing for larger ∑ m ν \sum m_{\nu} is one of the most well-motivated extensions of the base model.
The normal hierarchy, in which the lowest two mass eigenstates have the smallest mass splitting, can give any ∑ m ν ≳ 0.06 ​ eV \sum m_{\nu}\ga 0.06\thinspace\text{eV} ; an inverted hierarchy, in which the two most massive eigenstates have the smallest mass separation, requires ∑ m ν ≳ 0.1 ​ eV \sum m_{\nu}\ga 0.1\thinspace\text{eV} . A constraint that ∑ m ν < 0.1 ​ eV \sum m_{\nu}<0.1\thinspace\text{eV} would therefore rule out the inverted hierarchy. For a review of neutrino physics and the impact on cosmology see e.g., Lesgourgues et al. 2013 . 
 As in 276 and 284 , we quote constraints assuming
three species of neutrino with degenerate mass, a Fermi-Dirac distribution,
and zero chemical potential. At Planck sensitivity the small mass
splittings can be neglected to good accuracy ( Lesgourgues & Pastor 2006 , see e.g.,) .
Neutrinos that become non-relativistic around recombination produce distinctive signals in the CMB power spectra,
which Planck and other experiments have already ruled out. If the neutrino mass is low enough that they became non-relativistic after recombination ( m ν ≪ 1 ​ eV m_{\nu}\ll 1\thinspace\text{eV} ), the main effect on the CMB power spectra is a change in the angular diameter distance that is degenerate with decreasing H 0 H_{0} . The Planck data then mainly constrain lower masses via the lensing power spectrum and the impact of lensing on the CMB power spectra. Since the CMB power spectra prefer slightly more lensing than in the base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model, and neutrino mass can only suppress the power, we obtain somewhat stronger constraints than might be expected in typical realizations of a minimal-mass neutrino model. 
 In 284 no preference for higher neutrino masses was found, but a tail to high neutrino masses was still allowed, with relatively high primordial amplitudes A s A_{\rm s} combining with high neutrino mass to give acceptable lensing power.
The tighter 2018 constraint on the optical depth from polarization at low multipoles restricts the primordial A s A_{\rm s} to be smaller, to match the same observed high- ℓ \ell power ( C ℓ ∝ A s ​ e − 2 ​ τ C_{\ell}\propto A_{\rm s}e^{-2\tau} ); this reduces the parameter space with larger neutrino masses, giving tighter constraints on the mass.
With only temperature information at high ℓ \ell , the 95 % CL upper bound moved from 0.72 eV ( 284 TT+lowP) to 0.59 eV (using the SimLow polarization likelihood of Planck Collaboration Int.
XLVI 2016 , at low ℓ \ell ) . This now further tightens to

 (56b) 
 Although the high- ℓ \ell TT spectrum prefers more lensing than in base Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} , the lensing reconstruction is very consistent with expected amplitudes.
In 284 , the 2015 lensing likelihood weakened joint neutrino mass constraints because it preferred substantially less lensing than the temperature power spectrum. The 2018 lensing construction gives a slightly (1–2 %) higher lensing power spectrum amplitude than in 2015, which, combined with the decrease in the range of higher lensing amplitudes allowed by the new TT+lowE likelihood, means that the constraints are more consistent.

 As discussed in Sect. 6.2 , the CamSpec TT,TE,EE+lowE likelihood shows a weaker
preference for higher lensing amplitude A L A_{\rm L} than the default Plik likelihood, and this propagates directly into a weaker constraint on the neutrino mass, since for small masses the constraint is largely determined by the lensing effect. The differences between Plik and CamSpec are much smaller if we add
CMB lensing, since the lensing measurements restrict the lensing amplitude to
values closer to those expected in base Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} . 
 The combination of the acoustic scale measured by the CMB ( θ MC \theta_{\rm MC} ) and BAO data is sufficient to largely determine the background geometry in the Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} + ∑ m ν \sum m_{\nu} model, since the lower-redshift BAO data break the geometric degeneracy. Combining BAO data with the CMB lensing reconstruction power spectrum (with priors on Ω b ​ h 2 \Omega_{\mathrm{b}}h^{2} and n s n_{\rm s} , following 286 ), the neutrino mass can also be constrained to be

 (59) 
 This number is consistent with the tighter constraints using the CMB power spectra, and almost independent of lensing effects in the CMB spectra; it would hold even if the A L A_{\rm L} tension discussed in Sect. 6.2 were interpreted as a sign of unknown residual systematics.

adding BAO data to the Planck likelihood significantly tightens the neutrino mass constraints. Without CMB lensing we find

 Increasing the neutrino mass leads to lower values of H 0 H_{0} , and hence aggravates the tension with the distance-ladder determination
of Riess et al. 2018a . Adding the Riess et al. 2018a 
 H 0 H_{0} measurement to Planck will therefore give even tighter neutrino mass constraints (see the parameter tables in the 273 ),
but such constraints should be interpreted cautiously until the Hubble tension is better understood. 
 Figure 34: Samples from Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE chains in the
 ∑ m ν \sum m_{\nu} – H 0 H_{0} plane, colour-coded by σ 8 \sigma_{8} .
Solid black contours show the constraints from Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing, while dashed
blue lines show the joint constraint from Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO, and
the dashed green lines additionally marginalize over N eff N_{\rm eff} .
The grey band on the left shows the region with ∑ m ν < 0.056 ​ eV \sum m_{\nu}<0.056\thinspace\text{eV} ruled out by neutrino oscillation experiments.
Mass splittings observed in neutrino oscillation experiments also imply that the region left of the dotted vertical line can only be
a normal hierarchy (NH), while the region to the right could be either the normal hierarchy or an inverted hierarchy (IH).
 The remarkably tight constraints using CMB and BAO data are comparable with the latest bounds from combining with Ly  α \alpha forest data ( Palanque-Delabrouille et al. 2015 ; Yèche et al. 2017 ) . Although Ly  α \alpha is a more direct probe of the neutrino mass (in the sense that it is sensitive to the matter power spectrum on scales where the suppression caused by neutrinos is expected to be significant)
the measurements are substantially more difficult to model and interpret than the CMB and BAO data.
Our 95 % 95\thinspace\% limit of ∑ m ν < 0.12 ​ eV \sum m_{\nu}<0.12\thinspace\thinspace\text{eV} starts to put pressure on the inverted mass hierarchy (which requires ∑ m ν ≳ 0.1 ​ eV \sum m_{\nu}\ga 0.1\thinspace\text{eV} )
independently of Ly  α \alpha data. This is consistent with constraints from neutrino laboratory experiments which also slightly prefer the normal hierarchy at 2 2 – 3 ​ σ 3\thinspace\sigma ( Adamson et al. 2017 ; Abe et al. 2018 ; Capozzi et al. 2018 ; de Salas et al. 2018a ; de Salas et al. 2018b ) .

Constraints on additional relativistic particles.
 Top :
Evolution of the effective degrees of freedom for Standard Model particle density, g ∗ g_{*} , as a function of photon temperature in the early Universe. Vertical bands show the approximate temperature of neutrino decoupling and the QCD phase transition, and dashed vertical lines denote some mass scales at which corresponding particles annihilate with their antiparticles, reducing g ∗ g_{*} . One-tailed 68 % 68\thinspace\% and 95 % 95\thinspace\% regions excluded by Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO are shown in gold; this rules out at 95 % significance light thermal relics decoupling after the QCD phase transition (where the theoretical uncertainty on g ∗ g_{*} is negligible), including specific values indicated on the right axis of Δ ​ N eff = 0.57 \Delta N_{\rm eff}=0.57 and 1 for particles decoupling between muon and positron annihilation. At temperatures well above the top quark mass and electroweak phase transition, g ∗ g_{*} remains somewhat below the naive 106.75 value expected for all the particles in the Standard Model, giving interesting targets for Δ ​ N eff \Delta N_{\rm eff} that may be detectable in future CMB experiments ( Baumann et al. 2018 , see e.g.) .
 New light particles appear in many extensions of the Standard Model of
particle physics.
Additional dark relativistic degrees of freedom are usually parameterized
by N eff N_{\rm eff} , defined so that the total relativistic energy density well after electron-positron annihilation
is given by 
 ρ rad = N eff ​ 7 8 ​ ( 4 11 ) 4 / 3 ​ ρ γ . \rho_{\rm rad}=N_{\rm eff}\ \frac{7}{8}\left(\frac{4}{11}\right)^{4/3}\ \rho_{\gamma}. 
 (62) 
 The standard cosmological model has N eff ≈ 3.046 N_{\rm eff}\approx 3.046 , slightly larger than
 3 3 since the three standard model neutrinos were not completely decoupled at
electron-positron annihilation ( Gnedin & Gnedin 1998 ; Mangano et al. 2005 ; de Salas & Pastor 2016 ) . 
 We can treat any additional massless particles produced well before
recombination (that neither interact nor decay) as simply an additional
contribution to N eff N_{\rm eff} . Any species that was initially in thermal equilibrium
with the Standard Model particles produces a Δ ​ N eff ( ≡ N eff − 3.046 ) \Delta N_{\rm eff}\thinspace(\equiv N_{\rm eff}-3.046) that depends only
on the number of degrees of freedom and decoupling temperature.
Using conservation of entropy, fully thermalized relics with g g degrees of freedom contribute 
 Δ ​ N eff = g ​ [ 43 4 ​ g s ] 4 / 3 × { 4 / 7 boson , 1 / 2 fermion , \Delta N_{\rm eff}=g\thinspace\left[\frac{43}{4\thinspace g_{s}}\right]^{4/3}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}4/7&{\rm boson},\\
1/2&{\rm fermion},\end{array}\right. 
 (63) 
 where g s g_{s} is the effective degrees of freedom for the entropy of the other thermalized relativistic species that are present when they decouple. 38 38 
 38 
 For most of the thermal history g s ≈ g ∗ g_{s}\approx g_{*} , where g ∗ g_{*} is the effective degrees of freedom for density, but they can differ slightly, for example during the QCD phase transition ( Borsanyi et al. 2016 ) . 
Examples range from a fully thermalized sterile neutrino decoupling at
 1 < ∼ T < ∼ 100 1\mathrel{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$<$\hss}\lower 5.16663pt\hbox{$\sim$}}}T\mathrel{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{$<$\hss}\lower 5.16663pt\hbox{$\sim$}}}100\thinspace MeV, which produces Δ ​ N eff = 1 \Delta N_{\rm eff}=1 , to a thermalized boson
decoupling before top quark freeze-out, which produces
 Δ ​ N eff ≈ 0.027 \Delta N_{\rm eff}\approx 0.027 . 
 Additional radiation does not need to be fully thermalized, in which case
 Δ ​ N eff \Delta N_{\rm eff} must be computed on a model-by-model basis.
We follow a phenomenological approach in which we treat N eff N_{\rm eff} 
as a free parameter. We allow N eff < 3.046 N_{\rm eff}<3.046 for completeness, corresponding to
standard neutrinos having a lower temperature than expected,
even though such models are less well motivated theoretically. 
 The 2018 Planck data are still entirely consistent
with N eff ≈ 3.046 N_{\rm eff}\approx 3.046 , with the new low- ℓ \ell polarization constraint lowering the 2015 central value slightly and with a corresponding
10 % reduction in the error bar, giving 
 N eff = 3.00 − 0.53 + 0.57 (95 %,  Planck  TT+lowE), \left.N_{\rm eff}=3.00^{+0.57}_{-0.53}\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{142.26378pt}{\begin{flushleft}(95\thinspace\%,\penalty\ {Planck}\ TT\allowbreak+lowE), \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (64a) 
 N eff = 2.92 − 0.37 + 0.36 (95 %,  Planck  TT,TE,EE+lowE), \left.N_{\rm eff}=2.92^{+0.36}_{-0.37}\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{142.26378pt}{\begin{flushleft}(95\thinspace\%,\penalty\ {Planck}\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE), \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (64b) 
 with similar results including lensing.
Modifying the relativistic energy density before recombination changes the sound horizon, which is partly degenerate with changes in the late-time geometry.
Although the physical acoustic scale measured by BAO data changes in the same way,
the low-redshift BAO geometry helps to partially break the degeneracies.
Despite improvements in both BAO data and Planck polarization measurements,
the joint Planck +BAO constraints remain similar to 284 : 
 N eff = 3.11 − 0.43 + 0.44 (95 %, TT+lowE+lensing+BAO); \left.N_{\rm eff}=3.11^{+0.44}_{-0.43}\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{156.49014pt}{\begin{flushleft}(95\thinspace\%,\penalty\ TT\allowbreak+lowE\allowbreak+lensing\allowbreak+BAO); \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (65a) 
 N eff = 2.99 − 0.33 + 0.34 (95 %, TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO). \left.N_{\rm eff}=2.99^{+0.34}_{-0.33}\quad\mbox{\text{\parbox{156.49014pt}{\begin{flushleft}(95\thinspace\%,\penalty\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE\allowbreak+lensing\allowbreak+BAO). \end{flushleft}}}}\right. 
 (65b) 
 For N eff > 3.046 N_{\rm eff}>3.046 the Planck data prefer higher values of the
Hubble constant and fluctuation amplitude, σ 8 \sigma_{8} , than for the
base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model. This is because higher N eff N_{\rm eff} leads to a
smaller sound horizon at recombination and H 0 H_{0} must rise to keep the
acoustic scale, θ ∗ = r ∗ / D M \theta_{*}=r_{\ast}/D_{\rm M} , fixed at the observed value. Since the change in the allowed Hubble constant with N eff N_{\rm eff} is associated with a change in the sound horizon, BAO data do not help to strongly exclude larger values of N eff N_{\rm eff} .
Thus varying N eff N_{\rm eff} allows the tension with Riess et al. 2018a to be somewhat eased, as illustrated in Fig. 35 . However, although the 68 % error from Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO on the Hubble parameter is weakened when allowing varying N eff N_{\rm eff} , it is still discrepant with R18 at just over 3 ​ σ 3\thinspace\sigma , giving H 0 = ( 67.3 ± 1.1 ) ​ km ​ s − 1 ​ Mpc − 1 H_{0}=(67.3\pm 1.1)\penalty\ \text{km}\penalty\ \text{s}^{-1}\text{Mpc}^{-1} . Interpreting this discrepancy
as a moderate statistical fluctuation, the combined result is 
 N eff = 3.27 ± 0.15 H 0 = ( 69.32 ± 0.97 ) ​ km ​ s − 1 ​ Mpc − 1 } 68 %, TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO+R18. \left.\begin{aligned} N_{\rm eff}&=3.27\pm 0.15\\
H_{0}&=(69.32\pm 0.97)\penalty\ \text{km}\penalty\ \text{s}^{-1}\text{Mpc}^{-1}\end{aligned}\ \right\}\ \ \mbox{\text{\parbox{85.35826pt}{\begin{flushleft}68\thinspace\%,\penalty\ TT,TE,EE\allowbreak+lowE\allowbreak+lensing\allowbreak+BAO\allowbreak+{R18.} \end{flushleft}}}} 
 (66) 
 However, as explained in 284 , this set of parameters requires an increase in σ 8 \sigma_{8} and a decrease in Ω m \Omega_{\mathrm{m}} , potentially
increasing tensions with weak galaxy lensing and (possibly) cluster count data. Higher values for N eff N_{\rm eff} also start to come into tension with
observational constraints on primordial light element abundances (see Sect. 7.6 ). 
 Restricting ourselves to the more physically motivated models with Δ ​ N eff > 0 \Delta N_{\rm eff}>0 , the one-tailed Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO constraint is Δ ​ N eff < 0.30 \Delta N_{\rm eff}<0.30 at 95 % 95\thinspace\% . This rules out light thermal relics that
decoupled after the QCD phase transition (although new species are still allowed if they decoupled at higher temperatures and with g g not too large).

 There are various theoretical scenarios in which it is possible to have both sterile neutrinos and neutrino
mass. We first consider the case of massless relics combined with the three

The parameters are not very correlated, so the mass constraint is similar
to that obtained when not also varying N eff N_{\rm eff} .

 The bounds remain very close to the bounds on either N eff N_{\rm eff} (Eq. 65b ) or ∑ m ν \sum m_{\nu} (Eq. 61b )
in 7-parameter models, showing that the data clearly differentiate between
the physical effects generated by the addition of these
two parameters. Similar results are found without

 The second case that we consider is massive sterile neutrinos combined with standard

described by Dodelson & Widrow 1994 . For low Δ ​ N eff \Delta N_{\rm eff} the physical mass
can therefore become large, in which case the particles behave in the same way as cold
dark matter. In our grid of parameter chains we adopt a prior that m sterile thermal < 10 ​ eV m_{\rm sterile}^{\rm thermal}<10\thinspace\text{eV} (and necessarily Δ ​ N eff ≥ 0 \Delta N_{\rm eff}\geq 0 ) to exclude parameter space that is

 (68b) 
 The mass constraint in Eq. ( 68a ) actually appears weaker than in
 284 ; this is because the change in optical depth reduces the high- N eff N_{\rm eff} 
parameter space, and the remaining lower- N eff N_{\rm eff} parameter space has significant
volume associated with models having relatively large m ν , sterile eff m_{\nu,\thinspace\mathrm{sterile}}^{\mathrm{eff}} 
(close to the m sterile thermal m_{\rm sterile}^{\rm thermal} prior cut). Removing this high-physical-mass
parameter space by tightening the prior to 2 ​ eV 2\thinspace\thinspace\text{eV} gives the mass constraint
in Eq. ( 68b ), which is substantially tighter than the result quoted in
 284 without high- ℓ \ell polarization. 
 One thermalized sterile neutrino with Δ ​ N eff = 1 \Delta N_{\rm eff}=1 is excluded at about
 6 ​ σ 6\thinspace\sigma irrespective of its mass, or at about 7 ​ σ 7\thinspace\sigma when
assuming a mass m sterile DW ≈ 1 ​ eV m_{\mathrm{sterile}}^{\mathrm{DW}}\approx 1\thinspace\thinspace\text{eV} .
This is especially interesting in the context of the controversial evidence for
light sterile neutrinos, invoked to explain the neutrino short baseline
(SBL) anomaly.
The latest MiniBooNE data on electron-neutrino appearance ( Aguilar-Arevalo et al. 2018 ) support previous anomalous results by LSND ( Aguilar-Arevalo et al. 2001 ) , with a combined significance of 6.1 ​ σ 6.1\thinspace\sigma in favour of electron-neutrino appearance.
However, this contradicts recent muon-neutrino
disappearance data from MINOS+ and IceCube ( Dentler et al. 2018 ) , when considered along with electron-antineutrino disappearance results ( Dentler et al. 2017 ; Gariazzo et al. 2018 ) , and also appears to be excluded by OPERA ( Agafonova et al. 2018 ) . The
long-standing evidence for electron-neutrino disappearance in reactor
experiments has also recently been challenged by new data from STEREO
 ( Almazán et al. 2018 ) and PROSPECT ( Ashenfelter et al. 2018 ) . It is worth
noting, however, that removing any individual experiment does not relieve
the tension between the remaining experiments, and mild tension still persists
if all electron (anti-)neutrino appearance (disappearance) data are removed
 (see Maltoni 2018 , for a detailed summary) .
Several analyses have shown that in order to fit the anomalous data sets with one sterile neutrino, one needs an active-sterile neutrino mixing angle such that the fourth neutrino mass eigenstate would acquire a thermal distribution in the early Universe ( Hannestad et al. 2013 ; Bridle et al. 2017 ; Knee et al. 2018 , see e.g.,) , thus contributing as Δ ​ N eff ≈ 1 \Delta N_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx 1 .  39 39 
 39 
 Note that Δ ​ N eff \Delta N_{\mathrm{eff}} could in principle be reduced if there was a small amount of lepton asymmetry in the early Universe; however, this would raise other types of problems ( Saviano et al. 2013 ) . Our
 Planck results confirm that the presence of a light thermalized sterile
neutrino is in strong contradiction with cosmological data, and that the
production of sterile neutrinos possibly explaining the SBL anomaly would need to be suppressed by some
non-standard interactions ( Archidiacono et al. 2016 ; Chu et al. 2015 ) ,
low-temperature reheating ( de Salas et al. 2015 ) , or another special mechanism. 
 7.6 Big-bang nucleosynthesis 
 7.6.1 Primordial element abundances 
 Primordial helium. The latest estimates of the primordial helium abundance come from the data compilations of Aver et al. 2015 , giving Y P BBN ≡ 4 ​ n He / n b = 0.2449 ± 0.0040 Y_{\text{P}}^{\rm BBN}\equiv 4n_{\rm He}/n_{\rm b}=0.2449\pm 0.0040 (68 % CL) and
 Peimbert et al. 2016 , giving a slightly tighter constraint Y P BBN = 0.2446 ± 0.0029 Y_{\text{P}}^{\rm BBN}=0.2446\pm 0.0029 (68 % CL). These two estimates are consistent with each other. Izotov et al. 2014 find a higher value, Y P BBN = 0.2551 ± 0.0022 Y_{\text{P}}^{\rm BBN}=0.2551\pm 0.0022 (68 % CL) in moderate (2.2 σ \thinspace\sigma to 2.9 σ \thinspace\sigma ) tension with the previous two.
 Aver et al. 2015 discuss the differences between their results and Izotov et al. 2014 , which are caused by modelling differences involving neutral hydrogen
collisional emission, corrections for dust absorption, and helium emissivities, amongst other effects. This raises the issue, which has long-plagued helium abundance measurements, of whether
the systematic errors are accurately incorporated in the quoted uncertainties. In this paper, we will use the more conservative Aver et al. 2015 results as the baseline; however,
we will occasionally quote bounds based on the combined Aver et al. 2015 and Peimbert et al. 2016 results ( Y P BBN = 0.2447 ± 0.0023 Y_{\text{P}}^{\rm BBN}=0.2447\pm 0.0023 (68 % CL)) and for
the Izotov et al. 2014 results. 
 Figure 38: Summary of BBN results with N eff = 3.046 N_{\rm eff}=3.046 , using Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE. All bands are 68 % credible intervals. The standard BBN predictions computed with PArthENoPE are shown in green (case (b) in the text), while those from PRIMAT are in black dashed lines (case (c)). The blue lines show the PArthENoPE results based on the experimental determination of nuclear rates by Adelberger et al. 2011 , instead of the theoretical rate of Marcucci et al. 2016 . 
 Compared to the measurement used in 284 , the Aver et al. 2015 error bar has decreased by a factor of 2.4.

the results from both codes

the helium abundance measurements and this tension should be borne in mind when we use the Aver et al. 2015 measurements
below. 
 Primordial deuterium. There has been significant progress related to deuterium abundance determination since the completion of 284 .
On the observational side, Cooke et al. 2018 have published a new estimate based on their best seven measurements in metal-poor damped Ly  α \alpha systems,
 y DP ≡ 10 5 ​ n D / n H = 2.527 ± 0.030 y_{\mathrm{DP}}\equiv 10^{5}n_{\rm D}/n_{\rm H}=2.527\pm 0.030 (68 % CL). On the calculational side, the value of the nuclear reaction rate d ​ ( p , γ ) 3 ​ He d(p,\gamma)^{3}\mbox{He} ,
which has a major impact on BBN computations of the primordial deuterium calculation, has now been calculated ab initio.
The most recent theoretical calculation is presented in Marcucci et al. 2016 and differs significantly
from previous predictions extrapolated from laboratory experiments by Adelberger et al. 2011 . This issue should be settled by forthcoming precise measurements by the LUNA experiment ( Gustavino 2017 ) . In this paper we will compare the results obtained when the deuterium fraction is computed in three different ways: 
 (a) 
 with PArthENoPE , assuming the experimental rate from Adelberger et al. 2011 ; 
 (b) 
 with PArthENoPE , using the theoretical rate of Marcucci et al. 2016 ; 
 (c) 
 with PRIMAT , using the rate from Iliadis et al. 2016 , based on a hybrid method
that consists of assuming the energy dependence of the rate computed ab initio by Marcucci et al. 2005 
and normalizing it with a fit to a selection of laboratory measurements. 
 In addition to the d ​ ( p , γ ) 3 ​ He d(p,\gamma)^{3}\mbox{He} reaction rates, the current
versions of PArthENoPE , PRIMAT , and other codes
 ( Nollett & Burles 2000 ; Nollett & Holder 2011 , such as that developed by) make
different assumptions on other rates, in particular those of the
deuterium fusion reactions d ​ ( d , n ) 3 d(d,n)^{3} He and d ​ ( d , p ) 3 d(d,p)^{3} H, which also
contribute significantly to the error budget of the primordial
deuterium fraction. PArthENoPE estimates these rates by averaging
over all existing measurements, while PRIMAT again uses a hybrid
method based on a subset of the existing data. When using one of approaches
(a), (b), or (c), we adopt different theoretical errors. For
(a), Adelberger et al. 2011 estimate that the error in their
extrapolated rate propagates to σ ⁡ ( y DP ) = 0.06 \sigma(y_{\mathrm{DP}})=0.06 . For (b),
we rely on the claim by Marcucci et al. 2016 that the error is now
dominated by uncertainties on deuterium fusion and propagates to
 σ ⁡ ( y DP ) = 0.03 \sigma(y_{\mathrm{DP}})=0.03 . For (c), the error computed by PRIMAT (close to the best-fit value of ω b \omega_{\mathrm{b}} ) is similar,
 σ ⁡ ( y DP ) = 0.032 \sigma(y_{\mathrm{DP}})=0.032 . 
 These systematic error estimates are consistent with the differences
between different BBN codes. Taking d ​ ( p , γ ) 3 ​ He d(p,\gamma)^{3}\mbox{He} 
from Marcucci et al. 2016 , the
prediction of PArthENoPE 1.10 is higher than that of the code by
 Nollett & Holder 2011 by about Δ ​ y DP = 0.04 \Delta y_{\mathrm{DP}}=0.04 , which is
comparable to the theoretical error adopted in this paper. Nollett & Holder 2011 
attribute this shift to their different assumptions on the deuterium
fusion rates. The shift between cases (b) and (c) is smaller, Δ ​ y DP = 0.015 \Delta y_{\mathrm{DP}}=0.015 , suggesting that differences in
 d ​ ( p , γ ) 3 ​ He d(p,\gamma)^{3}\mbox{He} and in the deuterium fusion rates nearly
compensate each other in the final result. 
 Nuclear rate uncertainties are critically important in the discussion
of the compatibility between deuterium measurements and CMB
data. Cooke et al. 2018 reported that their measurement of
primordial deuterium was in moderate 2.0 σ \thinspace\sigma tension with
the Planck baryon density from 284 . This is based on the
predictions of the code of Nollett & Holder 2011 with the nuclear rate
of Marcucci et al. 2016 .
Switching to PArthENoPE (b) and including the theoretical error
 σ ⁡ ( y DP ) = 0.03 \sigma(y_{\mathrm{DP}})=0.03 , we find consistency to
1.1 σ \thinspace\sigma . With our three BBN calculation pipelines, the deuterium abundance measurement of Cooke et al. 2018 translates into the following bounds on ω b \omega_{\mathrm{b}} , 
 ( a ) ​ ω b = 0.02270 ± 0.00075 ( b ) ​ ω b = 0.02198 ± 0.00044 ( c ) ​ ω b = 0.02189 ± 0.00046 }

&\mathrm{(c)\ }\thinspace\thinspace\omega_{\mathrm{b}}=0.02189\pm 0.00046\end{aligned}\ \right\}\ \ \mbox{\text{\parbox{113.81102pt}{\begin{flushleft}95\thinspace\%,\penalty\ \\

 (71) 
 including theoretical errors. In several places in this work and in 294 , we refer to a “conservative BBN prior,” ω b = 0.0222 ± 0.0005 \omega_{\mathrm{b}}=0.0222\pm 0.0005 (68% CL), set to be compatible with each of these three predictions. 
 We now update this discussion using the latest Planck results. With our three assumptions (a), (b), and (c) on standard BBN, the determination of ω b \omega_{\mathrm{b}} by Planck 2018 for the base- Λ \Lambda CDM model (see Eq. 69 ) implies

Thus no significant tensions are found in any of these cases. 
 Other light elements. We do not discuss other light elements, such as tritium and lithium, since
the observed abundance measurements and their interpretation in terms of the standard models of BBN are more
controversial (see Fields 2011 ; Fields et al. 2014 , for reviews) . The Planck results
do not shed any further light on these problems compared to earlier CMB experiments.

 Nuclear rates from bounds from Planck. The previous paragraphs highlighted the importance of assumptions on the radiative-capture process d ​ ( p , γ ) 3 ​ He {\rm d}({\rm p},\gamma)^{3}\mbox{He} for deuterium abundance predictions. It is worth checking whether the comparison of CMB and deuterium abundance data provides an indirect estimate of this rate. This approach was suggested in Cooke et al. 2014 and implemented in Di Valentino et al. 2014 and 284 . We can now update it using the latest Planck and deuterium data. 
 We parameterize the thermal rate R 2 ​ ( T ) R_{2}(T) of the d ​ ( p , γ ) 3 ​ He {\rm d}({\rm p},\gamma)^{3}\mbox{He} process in the PArthENoPE code by rescaling the rate R 2 ex ​ ( T ) R_{2}^{\rm ex}(T) fitted to experimental data by Adelberger et al. 2011 with a factor A 2 A_{2} : 
 R 2 ​ ( T ) = A 2 ​ R 2 ex ​ ( T ) . R_{2}(T)=A_{2}\thinspace R_{2}^{\rm ex}(T)\thinspace. 
 (73) 
 This factor does not account in an exact way for the differences between
the experimental fit and the theoretical predictions; it should instead be seen as a consistency parameter, very much like A L A_{\rm L} for CMB lensing in Sect. 6.2 . The rate R 2 th ​ ( T ) R_{2}^{\rm th}(T) predicted by Marcucci et al. 2005 has a temperature dependence that is close to what is measured experimentally, and can be very well approximated by a rescaling factor A 2 = 1.055 A_{2}=1.055 . The new theoretical rate obtained by Marcucci et al. 2016 has a slightly different temperature dependence but is well approximated
by an effective rescaling factor A 2 th = 1.16 A_{2}^{\rm th}=1.16 (Mangano & Pisanti, private communication). 
 Assuming the base- Λ ​ CDM \rm{\Lambda CDM} model, we then constrain A 2 A_{2} using Planck data combined with the latest deuterium abundance measurements from Cooke et al. 2018 . We still need to take into account theoretical errors on deuterium predictions arising from uncertainties on other rates, and from the difference between various codes. According to Marcucci et al. 2016 and Pitrou et al. 2018 , the deuterium fusion uncertainties propagate to an error σ ⁡ ( y DP ) = 0.03 \sigma(y_{\rm DP})=0.03 , which encompasses the difference on deuterium predictions between PArthENoPE versus PRIMAT . Thus we adopt σ ⁡ ( y DP ) = 0.03 \sigma(y_{\rm DP})=0.03 as the theoretical error on deuterium predictions in this analysis. Adding the theoretical error in quadrature to the observational error of Cooke et al. 2018 , we obtain a total error of σ ⁡ ( y DP ) = 0.042 \sigma(y_{\rm DP})=0.042 on deuterium, which we use in our joint fits of Planck +deutrium (D) data. We find

The situation is thus inconclusive and highlights the need for a precise experimental determination of the d ​ ( p , γ ) 3 ​ He {\rm d}({\rm p},\gamma)^{3}\mbox{He} rate with LUNA ( Gustavino 2017 ) .

modest increase in uncertainties. There is therefore only very limited

with the observed helium abundances of Aver et al. 2015 .

 7.7 Recombination history 
 The cosmological recombination era marks an important phase in the
history of the Universe, determining precisely how CMB photons
decoupled from baryons around redshift
 z ≈  10 3 z\thinspace{\approx}\thinspace 10^{3} ( Sunyaev & Zeldovich 1970 ; Peebles & Yu 1970 ) . With precision
data from Planck , we can test physical assumptions of the
recombination process ( Hu et al. 1995 ; Seljak et al. 2003 ) , studying both
standard and non-standard physics. 
 The Planck data are sensitive to several subtle atomic physics and
radiative-transfer effects ( Chluba & Sunyaev 2006 ; Kholupenko et al. 2007 ; Switzer & Hirata 2008 , see e.g.,) that were omitted in earlier calculations of the
recombination history ( Zeldovich et al. 1968 ; Peebles 1968 ; Seager et al. 2000 ) . These effects can lead to significant biases to several
cosmological parameters ( Rubiño-Martín et al. 2010 ; Shaw & Chluba 2011 , e.g.,) ; however,
as the Planck 2015 analysis confirmed, at the present level of
precision these can be reliably incorporated within the advanced
recombination codes CosmoRec ( Chluba & Thomas 2011 ) 
and HyRec ( Ali-Haïmoud & Hirata 2010 ) , as well as the recfast code ( Seager et al. 1999 ; Wong et al. 2008 ) , modified using corrections
calculated with the more precise codes. 
 In this section, we update the 284 search for deviations from
the standard recombination history. In particular, improved
polarization data provide additional constraining power that warrants
revisiting this question. As in 2015, we find no significant
indication for departures of the recombination history from the
standard prediction. 
 We use a semi-blind eigen-analysis (often referred to as a
principal-component analysis) of deviations of the free-electron
fraction, x e ​ ( z ) = n e / n H x_{\rm e}(z)=n_{\rm e}/n_{\rm H} , where n H n_{\rm H} 
denotes the number density of hydrogen nuclei, away from the standard
recombination history ( Farhang et al. 2012 ; Farhang et al. 2013 ) . Specifically,
a perturbation, δ ​ x e ​ ( z ) / x e fid ​ ( z ) \delta x_{\rm e}(z)/x_{\rm e}^{\rm fid}(z) , is
expanded in N z = 80 N_{z}=80 bands of δ ​ z \delta z , spanning redshifts from well
before helium recombination to well past hydrogen recombination (taken
to be 200 ≤ z ≤ 3500 200\leq z\leq 3500 ). Here, x e fid ​ ( z ) x_{\rm e}^{\rm fid}(z) describes
the ionization history, assuming the standard recombination physics
and using the best-fitting cosmological parameters from Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE. 
 We then form the Fisher information matrix for the N z + N std + N nuis N_{z}+N_{\rm std}+N_{\rm nuis} parameters, corresponding to the
 x e x_{\rm e} -perturbation, standard cosmological, and nuisance
parameters, respectively. The Fisher matrix is then inverted to obtain
the parameter-parameter correlation matrix. Our focus is on the
 N z × N z N_{z}\times N_{z} block of this Fisher inverse, containing the
marginalized errors and correlations of the x e x_{\rm e} parameters.
The x e x_{\rm e} block is diagonalized, and the corresponding diagonal
variances are rank-ordered from the lowest to highest fluctuation
variance (i.e., from the best to worst constrained mode). The rotation
diagonalizing the Fisher inverse defines the x e x_{\rm e} 
eigenmodes. Truncation of the eigenmode hierarchy to determine the
number of x e x_{\rm e} modes used for parameter estimation is performed
according to some suitably chosen selection criterion. We refer to
these modes as “eXeMs”, the first three of which are shown in
Fig. 42 . Only a small number are probed by Planck 2018
data, even with the addition of the higher quality polarization
information. If instead we diagonalized the N z N_{z} block of the Fisher
matrix before inverting, the modes would be characterized by the fixed
best-fitting cosmological and nuisance parameter values, i.e., they
would not be marginalized. Those x e x_{\rm e} modes differ from the
eXeMs, but would give similar results ( Farhang et al. 2012 , as discussed
in) . 
 Figure 42: First three normalized x e x_{\rm e} modes constructed using the Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE likelihood. The modes are marginalized over standard and
nuisance parameters. The forecast measurement uncertainties for the mode amplitudes are σ μ 1 = 0.16 \sigma_{\mu_{1}}=0.16 , σ μ 2 = 0.23 \sigma_{\mu_{2}}=0.23 , and σ μ 3 = 0.73 \sigma_{\mu_{3}}=0.73 . The position and width of the Thomson visibility function are indicated by the error bars at the bottom of the figure. 
 For our analysis, we use the eXeMs, applying them to
the Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE+lensing+BAO data combination. By construction,
these modes are orthogonal to each other; 43 43 
 43 
 In practice, our
mode generation method gives slight mode correlations at the level of
3–9 % due to the numerical procedure and smoothing of the
mode-functions (see Farhang et al. 2012 , for details) . however,
correlations arise once the standard and nuisance parameters are
varied. This slightly modifies the errors and can also cause small
parameter biases ( Farhang et al. 2012 ) . Although the lowest order
 x e x_{\rm e} modes given in 284 look similar to those for the
2018 data, the precision of the Planck data requires the
eigen-analysis to be updated around the new fiducial point in
parameter space; indeed, we find subtle differences, e.g., a small
shift in the position of the first mode, to which the data are
sensitive. 
 Figure 43: Power spectrum responses to the first three x e x_{\rm e} modes, constructed using Planck TT,TE,EE+lowE, shown in Fig. 42 . For each curve, the corresponding x e x_{\rm e} mode was added to the standard recombination history with an amplitude corresponding to their predicted 1 ​ σ 1\thinspace\sigma uncertainties (i.e., σ μ 1 = 0.16 \sigma_{\mu_{1}}=0.16 , σ μ 2 = 0.23 \sigma_{\mu_{2}}=0.23 , and σ μ 3 = 0.73 \sigma_{\mu_{3}}=0.73 for the first three eXeMs).
